# SPACE MODELS : ARCHITECTURE -A WARE CONVER -

## ABSTRACT 

Modern state space models (SSMs), such as Mamba2, provide a compelling alter-native to transformers by combining linear-time sequence modeling with recurrent state-space dynamics. However, the behavior of SSMs in distributed learning settings remains poorly understood. In particular, the existing standard federated learning methods are largely architecture-agnostic, and do not account for the stability, selectivity, and state-space parameterization that characterize modern selective SSMs. To address this, we derive architecture-aware gradient and smooth-ness bounds for single- and multi-layer selective SSMs, and convergence bounds for FedAvg and FedProx, characterizing how recurrent stability, input-dependent discretization, and state projection norms affect federated optimization. We then numerically validate the single-layer bounds on sequences generated by a teacher SSM, using a learner that follows the analyzed recurrence. We use this analysis to formulate expectations about the effects of local training and client heterogeneity, and examine these expectations by comparing nine federated learning algorithms on Mamba2 language modeling across six text domains. These experiments illus-trate how SSM-specific bounds can provide a basis for interpreting the behavior of practical federated learning algorithms. 

## 1 INTRODUCTION 

The modern generative AI landscape is largely dominated by transformer architecture (Vaswani et al., 2017), with the majority of the state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) employing them (Agarwal et al., 2025; Team et al., 2026a; Qiu et al., 2026; Team et al., 2026b). Despite this widespread success, transformers face critical limitations in certain scenarios, often driven by the core efficiency bottleneck of their self-attention mechanism, scaling quadratically as O(L2) with sequence length L in both time and memory complexity (Tay et al., 2022). To address this inefficiency, a variety of alternative mechanisms and architectures have been proposed, including sparse attention (Child et al., 2019; Zaheer et al., 2020), linear attention approximations (Katharopoulos et al., 2020; Choromanski et al., 2021), and modernized recurrent frameworks (Peng et al., 2023). Among these innovations, state space models (SSMs) (Gu et al., 2022a) have emerged as one of the most promising new paradigms. These modern SSMs, such as Mamba (Gu and Dao, 2024), overcome the quadratic bottleneck by operating with a strictly linear-time complexity, O(L), during training, and a constant-sized latent state, O(1) .Driven by these computational advantages, the SSM architecture has undergone rapid development, achieving competitive performance at a reduced computational cost compared to transformers. Archi-tectural innovations, such as the Structured State Space (S4) sequence model, HiPPO initialization (Gu et al., 2022a), and selective mechanisms (Gu and Dao, 2024) have enabled SSMs to dynamically filter information and capture and retain long-range dependencies. Most recently, SSMs have demon-1arXiv:2610.02659v2 [cs.LG] 6 Oct 2026 Preprint. Under review. strated capacity for achieving state-of-the-art language modeling performance at scale (Lahoti et al., 2026; Zuo et al., 2024; Blakeman et al., 2025; Team et al., 2024). 

Research Gap. Despite these advancements, the deployment of SSMs in distributed learning envi-ronments, where training is conducted across a set of agents, remains largely unexplored. While some studies have considered the intersection of SSMs with federated learning (FL) (McMahan et al., 2017), most of these frameworks are architecture-agnostic . Conversely, existing SSM-specific distributed learning studies either focus on classical, non-selective SSM settings (Kec ¸eci et al., 2025), or use SSMs for specialized personalized-FL applications (Zhang et al., 2026), leaving the convergence behavior of modern selective SSMs largely uncharacterized. Developing a foundational understanding of selective SSM convergence behavior in distributed settings can enable architecture-aware parameter tuning, protocol selection, and other control tasks for training performance optimization, uncovering another advantage of SSMs over transformer LLM approaches that rely on architecture-agnostic optimization tools (Fang et al., 2026; Soro et al., 2026). We thus pose the following question: 

How can we leverage the structural properties of modern selective SSMs to derive architecture-aware convergence bounds that rigorously characterize training behavior in terms of core state space parameters for distributed and federated learning techniques? 

Contributions. In answering this question, our main contributions are summarized as follows: • Convergence analysis for distributed selective SSMs: We provide an architecture-aware gradient norm bound for selective SSMs, revealing the impact of SSM parameters like core state-space parameter blocks. Then, we use this bound to establish the convergence rate of popular FL algorithms training selective SSMs, showing dependence on standard distributed learning factors like smoothness as well as architecture-specific parameters like block-wise spectral-norm envelopes. To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to provide an architecture-aware convergence rate of FL algorithms for selective SSMs. • SSM theory validation and theory-motivated algorithm comparison : We validate our theo-retical results using teacher-model derived sequences and single-layer selective SSMs, observing no gradient-bound violations, increased client disagreement as ρ approaches one, and increased drift with more local steps. We use the theoretical takeaways to rank federated algorithms on heterogeneous-domain datasets. Our results show that these rankings highly correlate with valida-tion performance, supporting the practical relevance of the SSM-specific quantities identified by our analysis. 

## 2 RELATED WORK 

State Space Models (SSMs). SSMs originate from classical control theory concepts, where they model dynamic systems through the evolution of latent states over time (Kalman, 1960). The modern SSMs were introduced with the pivotal Structured State Space (S4) (Gu et al., 2022a) and its variants (Gu et al., 2022b; Hasani et al., 2023; Gu et al., 2020), and leverage structured parameterizations to efficiently capture long-range dependencies. Selective SSMs introduced input-dependent state-space parameters to dynamically filter information (Gu and Dao, 2024) while the Structured State Space Duality (SSD) established connections with linear attention and enabled efficient implementations (Dao and Gu, 2024), enabling better expressivity, training stability, and hardware-aware optimizations (Hwang et al., 2024; Kim et al., 2026; Lahoti et al., 2026). These advances have driven growing adoption across language modeling (Waleffe et al., 2024; Blakeman et al., 2025; He et al., 2025; Glorioso et al., 2024; Ren et al., 2025), computer vision (Li et al., 2024; Zhu et al., 2024; Xie et al., 2025), and other sequence modeling applications (Ma et al., 2025). 

Distributed Learning with SSMs. Distributed learning enables model training across computational nodes, with federated learning (FL) addressing settings where data remain distributed among clients. Since the introduction of a foundational federated learning algorithm - FedAvg (McMahan et al., 2017) - numerous methods have been proposed to address statistical heterogeneity, communication costs, and client drift (Li et al., 2020; Karimireddy et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2020; Reddi et al., 2021; Acar et al., 2021; Li et al., 2021). However, despite the growing adoption of SSMs, theoretical understanding of their behavior in these settings remains limited. The existing SSM-related approaches address specific challenges: FedAlign (Kec ¸eci et al., 2025) tackles the problem of models converging to different parameter basins in federated system identification, while FedSSM (Zhang et al., 2026) 2Preprint. Under review.  

> (a) Selective SSM layer (b) Distributed SSM training

Figure 1: (a) A selective SSM layer generates input-dependent discretization parameters from xt and uses them to update the hidden state ht and output yt. (b) Federated SSM training proceeds over communication rounds: the server broadcasts W r , clients perform E local updates on local datasets 

Dk, and the resulting models are aggregated into W r+1 .uses SSMs as a meta-learning tool within personalized FL. Related works also explore merging operations on hidden states, contexts, or representations (Liu et al., 2025; Pi´ oro et al., 2024), rather than weight-space aggregation of independently trained selective SSMs. As a result, there remains an open question of how selective SSM dynamics affect optimization and aggregation guarantees in distributed learning settings. This motivates our study of connecting the architectural properties of selective SSMs to their behavior in distributed training. 

## 3 SYSTEM MODEL 

State Space Model. We consider a decentralized learning environment operating over a global selective state space model (SSM) parameterized by W = ( WB , W C , W ∆, A ) where W∆ ∈ RD×D ,

WB ∈ RN ×D, WC ∈ RN ×D, and A ∈ RN ×1, with A representing diagonal entries of a structured matrix A = diag (A). The SSM architecture is inspired by classical control theory concepts, in particular modeling dynamic systems through the evolution of latent states over time. These systems map a continuous input signal x(t) ∈ R to an output sequence y(t) ∈ R through an evolving, compressed latent state h(t) ∈ RN using linear time-invariant (LTI) differential equations: 

h′(t) = Ah(t) + Bx (t) (1a) y(t) = Ch (t) (1b) However, since neural networks process discrete sequences, these continuous-time SSM dynamics are mapped to a discrete recurrence using a discretization rule. Under this discrete formulation, the model maps an input sequence x = ( x1, . . . , x S ) where xt ∈ RD×1 to a sequence of outputs 

yt ∈ RD×1 through a sequence of compressed latent states ht ∈ RN ×D . While earlier foundational models utilized various discretization rules (Gu et al., 2022a), we adopt the first-order exponential-Euler discretization used in the more recent architectures (Lahoti et al., 2026). This discretization scheme retains the exponential state transition while applying a first-order approximation to the input contribution. We analyze the discrete model defined by this recurrence. In addition to this discrete foundation, modern SSMs introduce a selective mechanism that enables dynamic filtering of relevant information across the input sequence context. The selective SSMs achieve this by making the state-space and time-scale parameters depend on the current input. Specifically, given the model weights W , the input-dependent SSM quantities corresponding to an input token xt at the sequence position t are defined as: 3Preprint. Under review. • Time-scale parameter: ∆t = Softplus (W∆xt), where ∆t ∈ RD×1, acts as a gating mechanism, controlling the discretization step size to determine importance of the current token. • State transition matrix: ¯At = exp( A∆⊤ 

> t

), where ¯At ∈ RN ×D, determines the retention and decay of the historical context, managing the system’s memory across the sequence. • Input matrix: ¯Bt := Bt∆⊤

= ( WB xt)(Softplus( W∆xt)) ⊤, where ¯Bt ∈ RN ×D, and Bt := 

WB xt. Under the adopted exponential-Euler discretization, this matrix modulates how strongly the current input vector influences the newly updated latent state. • Output matrix: Ct = WC xt, where Ct ∈ RN ×1, linearly projects the compressed latent state back into the target output space. Given these dynamically generated matrices, the discrete hidden state sequence ht and the corre-sponding output yt at each sequence step are computed according to the following recurrence: 

ht = ¯At ⊙ ht−1 + ¯Btdiag (xt) (2a) yt = h⊤

Distributed Optimization Environment. Given the selective SSM setup established above, we consider the deployment of this architecture in a decentralized learning setting. In this framework, a central server initializes a global selective SSM model parameterized by weights W 0, which is collaboratively trained across a network of K clients. Each client k ∈ { 1, . . . , K } possesses a local dataset Dk = {ξk,i ∼ P k}|D k |

To solve this optimization problem, the framework employs a decentralized training procedure initialized with the global model W 0. The optimization then proceeds over discrete communication rounds r ∈ T := {0, 1, . . . , T − 1}. At each round r, the server shares the current global parameters 

W r with the participating clients. Each client k initializes its local model as W r, 0

The specific formulation of Fk(W ) depends on the distributed learning algorithm employed. For instance, in the classical FedAvg algorithm (McMahan et al., 2017), the local objective of client k is the expected sequence loss, i.e., Fk(W ) := Lk(W ). In contrast, FedProx (Li et al., 2020) modifies the local objective as F rk (W ) := Lk(W ) + μ

Through this local optimization step, the features of different distributions Pk are implicitly embedded into the updated local weights, resulting in a set of specialized models W r,E k . ## 4 THEORETICAL ANALYSIS 

Given this decentralized SSM setup, we consider distributed training procedures that update a global model through repeated local adaptation and server-side aggregation. These procedures induce a global optimization trajectory {W r }Tr=0 over the objective defined in (4). Since f is generally nonconvex, we characterize convergence through the standard expected gradient stationarity criterion 

> 1
> T

PT −1 

> r=0

E ∥∇ f (W r )∥2 

> F

.

The central problem is to derive convergence bounds that explicitly capture the structure of selective SSMs, rather than relying solely on architecture-agnostic smoothness, variance, and heterogeneity constants. In particular, the recurrent state update induce dependencies between the local optimization trajectory and SSM-specific quantities including the stability of the discretized transition matrices, the input-dependent time scale parameter ∆t = Softplus( W∆xt), and the norms of the state-space projection parameters. Our convergence analysis characterizes how these SSM-specific quantities control gradient growth, client drift, and aggregation error. As such, in the remainder of this section, we establish theoretical guarantees for federated training of selective SSMs. We first derive an explicit gradient norm bound for a single-layer selective SSM and use it to characterize federated optimization in terms of SSM-specific parameters. We provide convergence guarantees for FedAvg and FedProx, and relate additional federated baselines to the resulting stability, parameter-sensitivity, and distribution-shift quantities. The main text highlights the key results and their implications, while full proofs, supplementary corollaries, and additional baseline analyses are provided in Appendices A, B, C, D, E. 4.1 GRADIENT NORM BOUND FOR SELECTIVE SSM S

The main technical challenge lies in controlling the parameter sensitivity induced by the input-dependent SSM recurrence. The loss gradient depends on the model parameters both directly through the output projection and indirectly through their influence on the recurrent hidden states. By bounding these two contributions separately and subsequently combining them, we derive a sequence-wise gradient bound that is independent of the sequence length. We first introduce the notation used in our analysis. An input-target pair ξ is admissible if ξ ∈ Z ,where Z is a fixed, parameter-independent measurable set of input-target sequences satisfying 

Pr ξ∼P k (ξ ∈ Z ) = 1 for all clients k. All conditions below hold uniformly for admissible sequences, finite lengths S ≥ 1, and token indices t ≤ S, with finite constants independent of S, ξ, and t. Next, we state the necessary definitions and assumptions on the loss and the input sequence. 

Definition 4.1 (SSM Stability) . For γ, δ > 0, define Sγ,δ as the set of parameter tuples W satisfying 

An ≤ − γ for every n ∈ { 1, . . . , N }, where An is the n-th element of the transition parameter A,and [Softplus( W∆xt)] d ≥ δ for every feature coordinate d ∈ { 1, . . . , D } and admissible input token. Consequently, every entry of ¯At satisfies:   ¯At

The key technical step is to bound both the hidden states and their parameter Jacobians under input-dependent transitions. The stability condition ρ = exp( −γδ ) < 1 allows us to reduce the recurrent contributions to geometric sums, yielding an explicit parameter-gradient bound rather than requiring such a bound as an assumption. The resulting architecture-aware bound is independent of the sequence length while retaining explicit dependence on ρ and the core selective SSM parameter blocks W∆, WB , WC , and A. The complete proof is provided in Appendix A. Appendix C extends the analysis to the multi-layer setting by combining stability with layerwise input-output sensitivities, resulting in the bound G(Q)SSM (W ) stated in Theorem C.3. Together, these results provide architecture-specific control of the gradient norm required for the subsequent federated convergence analysis. 4.2 CONVERGENCE ANALYSIS

With these conditions in place, we characterize the convergence of FedAvg for selective SSMs. The corresponding analysis for FedProx and supplementary corollaries are available in Appendix B, D. 

Corollary 4.8 (Single-layer FedAvg convergence rate) . Under Assumptions 4.2- 4.6, the conditions specified in Definitions 4.1- 4.5, and the conditions of Theorem B.5, let MB , M C , M ∆, and MA

The quantities N, D, L x, L ℓ, and Hℓ affect only the implicit constant and do not alter the displayed scaling with the optimization parameters, parameter envelopes, stability, or sequence length. Proof sketch and discussion. Standard FedAvg convergence bounds assume smoothness and bounds on stochastic gradient variance and client-gradient disagreement. In contrast, our analysis derives these quantities from the selective SSM recurrence. We first bound the hidden states and their parameter derivatives to obtain the gradient bound. The second-order analysis then establishes local smoothness, while the remaining lemmas translate the gradient bound into bounds on stochastic variance and client disagreement. Together, these results yield a convergence rate whose model dependence is explicit in the parameter envelopes MB , M C , M ∆, M A and the stability factor ρ, with dimensions and input and loss regularity constants fixed. As a result, the corollary identifies which SSM parameters enter the convergence guarantee without requiring a separately assumed smoothness or gradient-dissimilarity constant for the model. The derived bound distinguishes sequence length from recurrent memory: the gradient and smooth-ness estimates contain no explicit sequence-length factor, but increase as the recurrent stability factor 

ρ approaches one, allowing past state information to persist for longer. This dependence is stronger in the additional local-drift terms, which contain M 6

> R

/(1 − ρ)12 , as opposed to the baseline stochastic term, which contains M 4

> R

/(1 − ρ)8. Consequently, the bound assigns a larger penalty to performing multiple local updates when ρ is close to one. However, ρ alone does not determine this penalty: even for the same ρ and client distributions, larger parameter envelopes increase the coefficient multiplying 

HTV . The analysis therefore separates the amount of distribution mismatch from the influence of the SSM parameters on its contribution to the convergence bound. This heterogeneity contribution vanishes at E = 1 , but does not decrease with batch size when E > 1. Our synthetic experiments observe increasing client disagreement as ρ approaches one, supporting this qualitative connection without establishing the worst-case exponents. Furthermore, the radius R determines how the broadcast model’s weight norms are enlarged to obtain bounds valid throughout local training: each block satisfies ∥WX ∥2 ≤ ∥ W rX ∥2 + R. As such, allowing greater client displacement increases the parameter envelopes entering the convergence bound, even when ρ remains unchanged. Additional local steps can therefore affect the guarantee through both the explicit dependence on E and the larger envelopes needed to cover the resulting trajectories. These trajectories must also remain within a neighborhood satisfying NR(W r ) ⊆ S γ,δ .The FedProx extension in Appendix B demonstrates how restricting client movement changes the guarantee: its effective radius Rμ ≤ R permits smaller envelopes M μX = MX − R + Rμ, reducing the SSM-dependent factors multiplying noise and heterogeneity. However, the proximal term also increases local smoothness by μ. The resulting bound therefore exposes a trade-off between tighter control of the SSM parameters and the additional smoothness cost of proximal regularization. 

## 5 EXPERIMENTS 

In this section, we empirically evaluate the derived bounds and their implications for distributed SSM training in two parts: (i) direct evaluation of gradient and smoothness bounds using sequences generated by frozen SSM teachers, and (ii) Mamba2 language modeling experiments with nine federated learning algorithms. In the latter, we compare theory-motivated expectations with observed performance illustrating the practical utility of the derived bounds. Details on our experimental setup are included in Appendix G, with supplementary experimental results available in Appendix H. 

Validation of Derived Bounds. We consider autoregressive token sequences generated by a frozen one-layer selective SSM parameterized by W = ( W∆, W B , W C , A ) analyzed in Theorem 4.4. This setup follows the formulation of Section 3 exactly, with the teacher SSM generating one-hot token (Lx = 1 ) sequences of length S = 64 for different combinations of D, N and stability limits ρ,yielding 72 distinct datasets. For each such dataset, we train a learner SSM on 4096 sequences using projected SGD with softmax cross-entropy loss ( Lℓ = √2), with the model updates inside Sγ,δ . Results and Discussion. No violations were detected in 1,411,776 gradient, smoothness and bound-variation checks (Figure 2a, Table 1), or in 103,200 federated checks across 1,200 FedAvg/FedProx runs on 72 teacher datasets. The federated runs explore step sizes beyond the theorems’ range. The results also reveal informative patterns. 

Memory amplifies gradient and curvature sensitivity. Across all generated datasets, the measured gradient and estimated Hessian norms grow approximately as (1 −ρ)−0.5 and (1 −ρ)−1.0, respectively, while their bounds grow as (1 − ρ)−1.8 and (1 − ρ)−3.6, as seen in Figure 2b. Although the bounds grow more steeply, the curvature exponent is twice the gradient exponent for both the measured quantities and the certified constants. These trends are consistent with the recurrent-sensitivity structure of Theorem 4.4 and Lemma 4.7. The shared sensitivity factor Lx(HR + MC JR) enters the gradient bound linearly and the first term of the smoothness bound quadratically in (129). The approximately doubled fitted curvature exponent mirrors this linear-versus-quadratic dependence, although the observed growth is substantially milder than that of the bounds. 

Recurrent sensitivity and block-wise gradients. The recurrent-Jacobian term, which propagates parameter perturbations through the transition ¯At, carries at least 99% of GSSM at every ρ. The measured gradient energy (the share of ∥∇ W L∥ 2 

> F

carried by each block) shifts from WB , W C (86% 

at ρ = 0 .3) to A (85% at ρ = 0 .999 , Figure 2c). Dimension fits (Figure 2d) both vary with ρ and give bound exponents of 1.4-2.9 in D and 1.1-2.0 in N , reflecting the N D 2-type factors in KW .Appendix H.1 examines the quantities entering the convergence analysis. The observed E0.87 growth of the ∥W r,E k − W r ∥F drift is consistent with the linear accumulation bound ηE GSSM ,R used in the FedAvg analysis. Table 2: Theorem-motivated expectations versus observed behavior on Mamba2. −/=/+ denote expected lower/similar/higher validation perplexity relative to FedAvg, motivated by the listed mechanisms. ∆PPL is the mean percentage change across the 24 configurations at each β.Across the bank, variation in log-utilization log 10 (∥∇L∥ F /GSSM ) is dominated by the memory factor 

ρ/ (1 − ρ)2. Its signed covariance shares are 93% for log-bound variance and 102% for log-utilization variance, the latter partly offset by measured gradient growth. Dimension and norm factors contribute less (Figure 9). This identifies the main source of variation in tightness, not absolute looseness. 

Limitations and outlook. The experiments did demonstrate some limitations of our derived bounds, mostly how conservative their values are in absolute terms, and how parameter-envelope growth with radius R over T rounds and saturation of the sequence-level total-variation metric affect the looseness. Nevertheless, the observations agree qualitatively with the analysis’s stability, sensitivity, and drift dependencies: no violations were detected, gradients and curvature grow with memory, blockwise gradients shift toward A, and drift grows with local steps. The recurrent factor ρ/ (1 − ρ)2 dominates variation in tightness across the bank, with smaller contributions from projection and input factors. As such, they point to concrete future improvements: data-dependent constants that use the realized stability margin and the finite sequence length, envelopes that track the actual local trajectories rather than the worst case over the whole radius R, and a token-level dissimilarity that would make the heterogeneity term sensitive to the data partition. The Mamba2 experiments that follow examine whether the same drift and heterogeneity mechanisms help interpret practical federated training. 

Practical Federated Optimization. We train randomly initialized Mamba2 (Dao and Gu, 2024) models of depth 4 and 16 across K = 4 clients for T = 50 rounds with E ∈ { 64 , 128 , 256 } local steps and heterogeneity β ∈ { 0, 0.7, 1} on six RedPajama (Together Computer, 2023) domains under four domain assignments ( β = 0 gives every client the same domain mix, β = 1 one domain each). Nine algorithms are compared over 72 configurations and three seeds using mean last-ten-round validation perplexity, which also selects their hyperparameters (see Appendix G for details). 

Theory-Guided Expectations. An update rule affects radius R (in GSSM ,R , L SSM ,R ), a heterogeneity term ∝ η2L2SSM ,R G2SSM ,R E(E − 1) HTV , and the server step to W r+1 . For SGD, FedProx bounds drift by Rμ ≤ ηE GSSM ,R (Theorem B.6 in Appendix B), at the cost of μ in Lprox ,R μ , motivating benefits from tuned proximal regularization. SCAFFOLD and FedDyn can reduce corrected client disagreement when corrections track client-gradient differences, though benefits may be smaller when disagreement is small. FedExP, FedAdam and DiLoCo rescale server updates and may enlarge neighborhoods, while the norm-preserving FedAlign and small-perturbation FedSAM may behave like FedAvg, without a shared convergence guarantee (Appendix E). Table 2 summarizes these expectations for the AdamW-based Mamba2 experiments motivated by the insights provided by our analysis. 

Results and Discussion. Across all 72 configurations and all three random seeds, the Pearson correlation between the expected performance direction (encoded as −1, 0, and +1 for lower, similar, and higher perplexity, respectively) and the observed log-perplexity change relative to FedAvg is 0.71 ,increasing to 0.81 when restricted to β = 1 .0. For the two heterogeneity regimes β ∈ { 0, 1}, the percentage changes in validation perplexity relative to FedAvg reported in Table 2, averaged over the 

24 configurations at each β and all three seeds, agree with 13 of the 16 expected lower/similar/higher outcomes. FedProx’s improvement over FedAvg is consistent with the potential benefit of tighter SSM envelopes identified in Theorem B.6 in Appendix B. SCAFFOLD’s improvement in 23 /24 

configurations at β = 1 , compared with deterioration in 22 /24 at β = 0 , aligns with the disagreement-9Preprint. Under review. correction interpretation of the FedAvg bound developed in Appendix E. Together, these results support using the theorem’s displacement and disagreement terms to interpret the observed depen-dence of algorithm performance on client heterogeneity. 

## 6 CONCLUSION 

In this work, we studied distributed learning with selective SSMs through architecture-aware theory, deriving bounds that connect federated optimization to recurrent stability, input-dependent discretiza-tion, projection norms, client drift, and heterogeneity. We then validated the derived bounds and provided experiments that demonstrated their relevance and practical applications. AI USE STATEMENT 

In this work, we used generative AI tools for: assisting in the writing of proofs, formulating mathematical claims (specifically, refining author-derived mathematical statements in response to feedback on proof correctness), proposing or refining hypotheses, designing or providing feedback on research methodology or experiments, implementing methods, supporting qualitative and thematic data analysis and interpreting results. We have not used generative AI tools for: helping develop theoretical models or conceptual frame-works, generating synthetic data sets, providing critical ingredients for proving mathematical claims, assisting with translation, or cleaning and reformatting a dataset. Additionally, we used generative AI tools for: creating or modifying scientific figures or images (limited to suggestions for figure titles and labels), editing a research paper to improve readability, and suggesting a structure for a research paper. Our use of generative AI was three-fold. First, generative AI tools were used to provide feedback on theoretical arguments, hypotheses, methodological choices, and interpretations of experimental results. For all proofs, theorems, corollaries, and other mathematical statements in this work, the underlying claims and arguments were first derived and formalized. The resulting statements and proofs were subsequently provided to an AI agent for feedback on their correctness, logical consistency, and the validity of the stated claims. The agent’s feedback was independently evaluated, and any resulting revisions were made manually. Similarly, generative AI tools were used to check the consistency of our experimental analyses and interpretations with the reported results. All final analyses, interpretations, and claims were determined by the authors. Second, AI agents assisted with the implementation of the code used in our experiments. While some experiments use synthetic data generated by deterministic code, AI agents were only utilized to assist with writing the code, not generate the synthetic data. All AI-assisted code was manually inspected for correctness and subsequently evaluated using validation tests before being used in the reported experiments. Third, we used large language models to improve the presentation of the manuscript, including wording, readability, organization, section flow, and textual elements such as figure titles. The scientific figures and images themselves were not generated or modified using generative AI (though their titles and labels were adjusted with suggestions from AI models). All suggested edits were reviewed by the authors and incorporated manually where appropriate. We have reviewed all AI-assisted work and take responsibility for the final content of this work, including text, claims, code, and other artifacts produced with the aid of generative AI. We provide complete proofs for the theoretical results presented in this work. For the empirical results, Appendix G provides detailed descriptions of the experimental setups, including the datasets and algorithms used, hyperparameter tuning procedures, and hardware used to conduct the experiments. ## B CONVERGENCE OF FEDERATED SSM B ASELINES 

In this section, we first define and prove a set of lemmas required to instantiate standard federated optimization convergence results in terms of selective SSM parameters. We then provide the proof of Theorem B.5, and state the corresponding FedProx theorem. B.1 AUXILIARY LEMMAS FOR SSM F EDERATED CONVERGENCE 

Rather than proving the FedAvg or FedProx convergence bounds directly, we first show how the generic smoothness, gradient second-moment, stochastic variance, and client heterogeneity quantities appearing in those bounds can be replaced by SSM-specific constants. In particular, we establish local control of the SSM gradient bound in Lemma B.1, prove the local smoothness constant LSSM ,R 

in Lemma 4.7, and derive the architecture-dependent quantities G2SSM ,R , σ2SSM ,R, Ξ, and Γ2SSM ,R in Lemmas B.2, B.3, and B.4, respectively. These lemmas are then used in the remainder of this section to state the final convergence bounds for FedAvg and FedProx distributed learning baselines. B.1.1 LOCAL LIPSCHITZ CONTINUITY OF THE SSM G RADIENT BOUND LEMMA AND PROOF 

Lemma B.1 (Local Lipschitz Continuity of the SSM Gradient Bound) . Let W r be some fixed reference parameter tuple and let 0 < R < ∞ be a finite radius such that NR(W r ) ⊆ S γ,δ . Suppose Assumptions 4.2 and 4.3 hold with the neighborhood-uniform constants specified in Section 4.2. 

21 Preprint. Under review. 

Let GSSM (W ) be the bounding function defined in Theorem 4.4. Then GSSM is Lipschitz continuous on NR(W r ).Specifically, with N, D, L ℓ, L x, γ, δ fixed, there exists a finite constant LGSSM ,R > 0, depending only on R and the block norms ∥W r

> ∆

∥2, ∥W rB ∥2, ∥W rC ∥2, and ∥Ar ∥2, such that for all W, V ∈ N R(W r ),

|G SSM (W ) − G SSM (V )| ≤ LGSSM ,R ∥W − V ∥F . (52) 

Proof of Lemma B.1: Fix the reference tuple W r and the radius R. Throughout this proof, 

N, D, L ℓ, L x, γ, δ are constant as W and V vary over the neighborhood NR(W r ). In particular, 

ρ = exp( −γδ ) is the same for both parameter tuples. First, by Theorem 4.4, GSSM (W ) has an explicit definition: 

GSSM (W ) = LℓLx KB K∆

√N D 

1 − ρ + ∥WC ∥2

KW

1 − ρ

!

, (53) where W is the tuple of selective SSM weights (W∆, W B , W C , A ).Since W = (W∆, W B , W C , A ) is a parameter tuple, given another parameter tuple V =(V∆, V B , V C , V A) we use the block Frobenius norm: 

∥W − V ∥2 

> F

:= ∥W∆ − V∆∥2

WA := A and W rA := Ar). Given the central model W r, we evaluate the maximum possible value of a norm of each parameter matrix within the neighborhood NR(W r ). Since W ∈ N R(W r ), each block satisfies 

∥WX − W rX ∥F ≤ ∥ W − W r ∥F ≤ R. (55) Therefore, by the triangle inequality and the fact that ∥ · ∥ 2 ≤ ∥ · ∥ F ,

∥WX ∥2 ≤ ∥ W rX ∥2 + ∥WX − W rX ∥2 ≤ ∥ W rX ∥2 + ∥WX − W rX ∥F ≤ ∥ W rX ∥2 + R. (56) Next, define:

## C MULTI -L AYER SELECTIVE SSM PROOFS 

In this section we derive the corresponding Gradient, FedAvg, and FedProx convergence bounds for a Selective SSM with of an arbitrary finite depth Q, extending our derivations in Sections A, and B to a multi-layer selective State Space Model (SSM) case. C.1 MULTI -L AYER SELECTIVE SSM G RADIENT BOUND PROOF

## D SUPPLEMENTARY COROLLARIES 

In this section, we provide supplementary corollaries to the theorems presented in the preceding sections and in the main manuscript. These corollaries simplify the preceding results and make explicit their dependence on SSM-specific quantities, including stability, parameter norms, network depth, and related architectural properties. ## E DISCUSSION ON ADDITIONAL DISTRIBUTED LEARNING FRAMEWORKS 

The FedAvg and FedProx results (Theorems B.5 and B.6) relate convergence of selective SSM training to recurrent stability, parameter norms, local client drift, and data heterogeneity. In this section, we use these quantities to interpret additional federated learning methods, identifying how their local updates and server aggregation interact with the SSM-specific bounds. While we do not establish convergence guarantees for these methods, the discussion provides a qualitative, theory-guided characterization of their convergence behavior, offering intuition and motivating expectations about their performance under different training conditions. The discussion below assumes SGD-style updates, containment in the stated neighborhood, and regularity assumptions. The connection to the AdamW-based Mamba2 experiments is therefore qualitative rather than a direct application of the convergence results. 

SCAFFOLD. SCAFFOLD (Karimireddy et al., 2020) introduces server and client control variates (correction terms), which act as drift-correction terms for local client updates. In its classical SGD form, the local update is 

W r,e +1  

> k

= W r,e k − η (gk(W r,e k ; ξr,e k ) + cr − crk) , (434) where cr = P 

> k

pkcrk approximates the global update direction and crk approximates the client-specific drift direction. More generally, allowing a correction scale λc > 0, define the corrected local direction 

dr,e k := gk(W r,e k ; ξr,e k ) + λc(cr − crk). (435) The correction term cr − crk is intended to reduce the component of the local update that points toward the client-specific objective rather than the global objective. Assume that the correction term satisfies: 

∥cr − crk∥F ≤ CR, (436) then the corrected direction satisfies: 

∥dr,e k ∥F ≤ GSSM ,R + λcCR := GSCAF ,R , (437) and the corresponding local drift radius can be bounded by 

RSCAF ≤ ηEG SCAF ,R . (438) While this mechanism does not directly change the SSM smoothness constant LSSM ,R , and could even increase the worst-case local drift radius through the additional correction term, the main effect of SCAFFOLD is to reduce the component of each client update that is biased toward the local objective rather than the global objective. In other words, SCAFFOLD can be interpreted as modifying the effective heterogeneity term. Specifically, define the corrected heterogeneity measure 

Γ2SCAF ,R := sup    

> W∈N R(Wr)
> K

X

> k=1

pk ∥∇L k(W ) − ∇ f (W ) + λc(cr − crk)∥2 

> F

. (439) When the control terms accurately estimate the client-specific drift directions, the correction λc(cr −

crk) partially cancels the difference ∇L k(W ) − ∇ f (W ). In this case, one expects 

Γ2SCAF ,R < Γ2SSM ,R . (440) 75 Preprint. Under review. This motivates a potentially reduced client disagreement, although a convergence bound must also account for the evolution and accuracy of the control variates. This does not imply that SCAFFOLD has a smaller drift radius than FedAvg. In fact, in nearly IID settings, the uncorrected client gradients are already approximately aligned with the global update direction. As a result, the reduction from Γ2SSM ,R to Γ2SCAF ,R may be limited, while the additional cor-rection term can increase the effective update radius and potentially degrade performance. In contrast, under non-IID data, the heterogeneity correction can provide a meaningful benefit by reducing the statistical drift term, which is amplified in the SSM-specific bound through the architecture-dependent gradient and smoothness quantities. 

FedAdam. FedAdam (Reddi et al., 2021) keeps the same FedAvg-style local client training phase, but replaces the direct server averaging step with an adaptive server optimizer. Let 

¯W r+1 =

> K

X

> k=1

pkW r,E k (441) denote the standard FedAvg aggregated parameters, and define the server pseudo-gradient: 

∆r := ¯W r+1 − W r . (442) FedAdam tracks moving averages of the server pseudo-gradient and its coordinatewise square as: 

mr = β1mr−1 + (1 − β1)∆ r , (443) 

vr = β2vr−1 + (1 − β2)(∆ r ⊙ ∆r ), (444) and updates the server model coordinatewise as 

W r+1 = W r + αs

mr

√vr + τs

, (445) where αs is the server learning rate and τs > 0 is the denominator offset. Since the client-side training phase is unchanged from FedAvg, FedAdam does not directly reduce the local SSM drift radius. The local client trajectories remain controlled by the same worst-case bound 

Rlocal ≤ ηEG SSM ,R . (446) The main change is instead introduced at the server: since √vr + τs ≥ τs, the server update satisfies the coarse bound 

∥W r+1 − W r ∥F ≤ αs

τs

∥mr ∥F . (447) As such, even though the adaptive server mechanism can be beneficial when it rescales poorly conditioned coordinates in a useful way, it can also increase the effective global update radius when  

> αs
> τs

is large or when the moment estimates are not well aligned with a stable descent direction. This can be particularly detrimental for selective SSMs - an aggressive server-side adaptive step can increase the effective neighborhood radius, resulting in larger potential values of LSSM ,R and 

GSSM ,R , and making the global trajectory more sensitive to non-IID client updates. Compared to FedProx, FedAdam does not explicitly constrain local client drift, and compared to SCAFFOLD, FedAdam does not correct the client heterogeneity term. Instead, it rescales the aggregated update after local drift has already occurred. 

FedAlign. The FedAlign baseline we consider is an adaptation of the state-alignment method proposed in (Kec ¸eci et al., 2025). The original FedAlign algorithm was developed for federated system identification with classical linear state-space models of the form 

˜xk+1 = ˜A˜xk + ˜Bu k, ˜yk = ˜C ˜xk + ˜Du k (448) where the system matrices ˜A, ˜B, ˜C, ˜D are fixed model parameters. In that setting, the main alignment issue is that different clients may learn dynamically equivalent SSMs in different state-coordinate bases, so directly averaging their parameters can distort the global system dynamics. FedAlign addresses this by using similarity transformations to map local SSMs into a common parameter basin before aggregation. 76 Preprint. Under review. Our setting differs from this original SYSID formulation because Mamba-style selective SSMs do not have a single fixed set of state-space matrices applied uniformly across the sequence. Instead, the effective transition and input terms are coupled to learned projections and input-dependent discretization quantities. Therefore, our FedAlign implementation adapts the same state-alignment principle to Mamba2 by aligning coupled SSM state, group, and head-indexed parameter blocks before aggregation. As such, we can write the adapted FedAlign aggregation as 

W r+1 =

> K

X

> k=1

pk Align k(W r,E k ), (449) where Align k is the client-specific alignment map applied to the coupled Mamba2 SSM blocks. Next, as long as Align k is a norm-preserving permutation or matching of coupled coordinates, then 

Align k(W r,E k ) − Align k(W r ) F

= W r,E k − W r

> F

. (450) The identity preserves distance when the same alignment is applied to both models, not distance to the fixed broadcast model W r. FedAlign can reduce aggregation error from mismatched state coordinates, but norm preservation alone does not control local drift, statistical heterogeneity, or stability-sensitive gradient growth in selective SSMs. FedAvg-like behavior is therefore expected when coordinate mismatch is limited. Containment of aligned models requires separate verification. 

FedDyn. FedDyn (Acar et al., 2021) combines proximal regularization with a dynamic gradient correction. In the full-participation, weighted formulation, client k maintains a state νrk , initialized at zero, and optimizes

FedSAM. FedSAM (Qu et al., 2022) retains FedAvg aggregation but uses a sharpness-aware local SGD step with two gradient evaluations: 

ur,e k := RSAM

NR+RSAM (W r ) ⊆ S γ,δ . The expanded neighborhood contains the perturbed evaluation points, resulting in the local-displacement bound

(457) As a result, relative to an SGD step from the same iterate and sample, SAM changes the update by at most ηL SSM ,R +RSAM RSAM . When the perturbation changes the SSM constants only slightly and its accumulated effect over training remains small, we expect FedSAM to perform similarly compared to FedAvg under both nearly IID and heterogeneous data. Note that this expectation relies on the small-perturbation approximation, and possible generalization benefits from flatter solutions are not quantified by these worst-case bounds. 

FedExP. FedExP (Jhunjhunwala et al., 2023) retains the FedAvg local updates but rescales the aggregated update:

≤ ηrg ηEG SSM ,R , (460) Since ηrg ≥ 1, this server-step upper bound is no smaller than the corresponding FedAvg bound, although the actual server displacement need not exceed the local radius. Therefore, server scaling may require a larger neighborhood to cover the server update, with larger parameter envelopes entering the SSM gradient and smoothness bounds. Containment of this expanded neighborhood in 

Sγ,δ is further not guaranteed. The frequency of rescaling depends on disagreement among the client updates, which can reflect both data heterogeneity and stochastic sampling. For IID data, we expect sufficiently similar client updates for the server scaling to remain mostly inactive, resulting in FedAvg-like behavior. However, given heterogeneous data, greater dispersion relative to the average update can activate scaling more often. The resulting server steps also amplify any local-drift error in the averaged update, without correcting client disagreement. For the considered SSM setting, we therefore expect worse performance than 78 Preprint. Under review. FedAvg when these displacement and stability costs outweigh the acceleration benefit. This is a qualitative expectation, not a convergence comparison. 

DiLoCo-style. The original DiLoCo framework (Douillard et al., 2024) combines AdamW local optimization with a Nesterov momentum server update. Our DiLoCo-style baseline retains the shared local phase and updates the server as follows: 

mro = βomr−1

(461) where αo > 0, βo ∈ [0 , 1) , and m−1

= 0 . Setting βo = 0 and αo = 1 reduces to FedAvg. For the SGD-based comparison, the same stable-neighborhood conditions with a common gradient bound GSSM ,R across rounds hold. Each averaged client update then has norm at most ηEG SSM ,R .As such, unrolling the server momentum recursion we get ∥mro∥F ≤ ηEG SSM ,R /(1 − βo), and therefore 

∥W r+1 − W r ∥F ≤ αo

1 − βo

ηEG SSM ,R . (462) When αo/(1 − βo) > 1, this upper bound exceeds the corresponding FedAvg bound. Furthermore, unlike FedExP, momentum can accumulate even when clients agree, because it combines averaged updates across rounds. Persistent update directions can increase server displacement, while changes in direction can leave the momentum contribution misaligned with the current averaged update. The server trajectory may therefore require a larger neighborhood and larger SSM parameter envelopes, with stable containment requiring separate verification. The within-round local update rule remains unchanged, and outer momentum does not directly correct client disagreement. As such, depending on the momentum parameters, the displacement and history effects could outweigh the acceleration benefit of momentum, leading to worse performance than FedAvg, although the effects could potentially balance out. 

Summary . The FedAvg bound (Theorem B.5) connects convergence to LSSM ,R , GSSM ,R , and 

HTV through local displacement, stable containment, and client disagreement. Under the stated assumptions, FedProx (Theorem B.6) and FedDyn’s proximal term damp displacement bounds, at a smoothness cost. SCAFFOLD and FedDyn can reduce corrected disagreement when their control states accurately track client-gradient differences, though IID data leave less disagreement to correct. FedAdam, FedExP, and DiLoCo-style retain the local phase but modify server updates through coordinatewise adaptation, disagreement-dependent extrapolation, and momentum, respectively. These updates may require larger stable neighborhoods. FedAlign targets coordinate mismatch through norm-preserving permutations, while FedSAM perturbs gradient evaluation points, so FedAvg-like behavior is expected when coordinate mismatch or accumulated perturbation effects, respectively, are small. These theorem-motivated expectations are collected in Table 2 and compared with observations in Section 5. 

## F DISCUSSION ON MODEL MERGING 

F.1 MODEL MERGING WITH SSM S MOTIVATION 

Section 3 formulates distributed learning as the minimization of the aggregated expected sequence loss in (4) through repeated local adaptation and parameter aggregation. The optimization proceeds over T communication rounds, each consisting of E local SGD steps followed by aggregation of the updated model parameters. This framework also provides a natural formulation of one-shot model merging (Wortsman et al., 2022; Ilharco et al., 2023). Consider the case of task or domain experts adapted from a common pretrained initialization. This setting shares the central operation studied in our distributed framework: combining parameter updates learned from heterogeneous data. Specifically, let M experts share the same selective SSM architecture, parameterization, and pretrained initialization W 0. Expert m adapts to a local 79 Preprint. Under review. dataset Dm associated with a distribution Pm, with all experts using the sequence-loss formulation of Section 3. To make the connection explicit, suppose each expert starts from W 0,0 

> m

= W 0 and performs E local SGD steps following the update rule in that section. Denote its final parameters and task vector by 

Wm := W 0,E m , Tm := Wm − W 0. (463) The independently adapted experts are then combined using a merging operator, 

Wmerge = Merge  {Wm}Mm=1 ; W 0 , (464) which may use both the expert parameters and the pretrained reference. If the merging operator is chosen to be weighted parameter averaging, the final model parameters are obtained as 

Wavg =

> M

X

> m=1

pmWm = W 0 +

> M

X

> m=1

pmTm, pm > 0,

> M

X

> m=1

pm = 1 , (465) which reduces exactly to one round of FedAvg with K = M and T = 1 when the weights in the objective and aggregation are kept the same. This demonstrates that averaging task vectors around a shared initialization provides a direct con-nection between one-shot model merging and the distributed optimization setting considered in this work. More general merging operators retain this sequence of independent adaptation followed by aggregation, although the differences in how the expert updates are combined limit the applicability of the current theoretical results. Additionally, practical model merging often involves large pretrained networks and extensive in-dependent fine-tuning. Although our formulation permits pretrained initialization and includes a multilayer extension in Appendix C, applying the results to complete pretrained architectures and expert-specific optimization protocols requires verifying additional assumptions. Applying our feder-ated convergence results to one-shot merging requires a neighborhood around W 0 containing the full independent adaptation trajectories, without intermediate synchronization. This may require a large radius R and correspondingly large parameter envelopes, increasing LSSM ,R and GSSM ,R and making the bounds loose or potentially uninformative. The required stability and step-size conditions may also become harder to satisfy. When a common stable neighborhood exists, convex averaging preserves containment within it, but stability alone does not guarantee predictive performance across the contributing domains. Nevertheless, the shared structure of independent adaptation followed by aggregation motivates including model merging as a related setting for interpreting our results. In the remainder of this section, we discuss connections between representative merging methods and our analysis, clarify their applicability and limitations, and highlight potential takeaways for merging selective SSMs. We further provide supplementary model merging results in Appendix H.3. F.2 MODEL MERGING METHODS : C ONNECTIONS TO SELECTIVE SSM A NALYSIS 

For a merged model parameterized by Wmerge with Wmerge ∈ N R(W 0) ⊆ S γ,δ , the uniform regularity conditions of Lemma 4.7 imply 

f (Wmerge ) = f  W 0 +  Wmerge − W 0

> Q

+

LℓΩQ is the existing multi-layer smoothness constant. These inequalities highlight important factors that determine the loss bound after merging. Firstly, the alignment of Wmerge − W 0 with −∇ f (W 0) indicates whether the update initially moves toward 80 Preprint. Under review. a lower aggregated loss. In addition, the merging operator determines the direction and magnitude of this update - different merging methods might rescale or modify the model parameters during aggregation. Finally, the SSM smoothness constant bounds the error in this local loss prediction and depends on the neighborhood over which it is evaluated. The radius R therefore plays two roles: it specifies the maximum displacement of the model parameters Wmerge − W 0 

> F

≤ R (or 

W (q)merge − W 0,(q)

> F

≤ Rq ∀q ∈ { 1, . . . , Q }) covered by the analysis, and determines the parameter range over which the smoothness bound holds. A larger radius can require larger parameter envelopes 

MX , leading to larger smoothness constants LSSM ,R (or L(Q)SSM ,RQ ) and a looser loss bound. This motivates examining how different merging methods affect the merged model’s displacement from W 0 and the neighborhood needed to contain it. We discuss several representative methods below. 

Weighted parameter averaging. If all the experts lie in a common stable neighborhood NR(W 0),their weighted average satisfies 

Wavg − W 0 

> F

≤

> M

X

> m=1

pm Wm − W 0 

> F

≤ R. (468) So an averaging merge operation does not enlarge the neighborhood containing the experts, and the same local smoothness bound applies. This property also holds for the multi-layer product neighborhood. Furthermore, Lemma B.4 bounds the weighted squared gradient disagreement at a common parameter tuple by 4G2SSM ,R HTV , showing how data heterogeneity and SSM sensitivity jointly determine this bound. 

Task Arithmetic. Task Arithmetic (Ilharco et al., 2023) scales combined task vectors. In a weighted, normalized form, 

WTA = W 0 + λT, T := 

> M

X

> m=1

pmTm, λ ≥ 0. (469) Here λ = 1 gives parameter averaging, while 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1 preserves containment in the common neighborhood. Larger scales require a separate containment check. In (466), the directional term scales with λ and the quadratic remainder with λ2. Thus, scaling controls displacement relative to SSM sensitivity, but shrinking the update can also suppress useful adaptation and cannot recover directions lost through cancellation. 

TIES-Merging. TIES-Merging (Yadav et al., 2023) trims small task-vector entries, elects each coordinate’s sign according to the total retained movement, and averages the nonzero entries agreeing with that sign. The resulting vector is scaled and added to W 0. Because coordinates may select different expert subsets, the result need not remain in the experts’ common neighborhood. Moreover, raw magnitude and sign do not account for how changes to A, W ∆, W B , and WC interact through the SSM recurrence. Our bounds therefore motivate sensitivity-informed trimming and conflict resolution, but do not establish that TIES improves stability or reduces the merged model’s loss. 

Practical Takeaways and Limitations. Overall, the model merging discussion motivates jointly considering expert adaptation, merge strength, and SSM sensitivity. The dependence of the bounds on neighborhood radii, recurrent stability, and interactions between layers motivates considering SSM-specific properties when evaluating and designing merging methods. Additionally, the derived relationships remain conditional on the stated assumptions - the local loss bounds require uniform regularity over a common convex stable neighborhood, while applying the federated convergence results additionally requires containment of the full adaptation trajectories. Extensive fine-tuning may therefore require large parameter envelopes and yield loose bounds. These limitations moti-vate extending the analysis to complete pretrained architectures, practical optimizers, and general merging operators as a promising direction for future work. We complement this discussion with supplementary model-merging experiments and an analysis of their results in Appendix H.3. 

## G DETAILED EXPERIMENTAL SETUP 

In this section, we describe the experimental setup in detail. The experiments fall into three groups: (i) synthetic dataset validation of the derived theoretical bounds, (ii) federated language modeling with 81 Preprint. Under review. practical selective state-space models on RedPajama, (iii) one-shot model merging supplementary experiments with pretrained Large Mamba2-based models. We detail the datasets, models, as well as training and validation procedures needed for reproducibility. G.1 DATASETS 

Synthetic Validation. The synthetic datasets are autoregressive token sequences sampled from frozen teacher selective SSMs that implement the recurrence of Section 3 exactly, so that every assumption of Theorem 4.4 holds for the data-generating model (rather than being assumed to hold). A teacher consists only of the four parameter blocks W = ( W∆, W B , W C , A ) of a layer (no embedding, convolution, residual path, normalization, MLP, or Transformer components). The inputs are one-hot encoded: xt is one of the standard basis vectors e1, . . . , e D of RD for every t. This formulation makes the input dimension D also act as the vocabulary size (since there are D different basis vectors). Since ∥xt∥2 = 1 , Assumption 4.3 holds with Lx = 1 .The teacher SSM is turned into a generative model as follows. For a partially generated sequence 

x1, . . . , x t, running it through the recurrence, starting from h0 = 0N ×D , produces an output yt ∈ RD ,which has one coordinate per token in the vocabulary. We therefore treat the d-th coordinate yt,d as the logit of the event that the next input is token d, i.e., that xt+1 = ed. We then take a softmax with temperature τ > 0 to convert these D logits into a probability vector over the tokens, and draw the next input as follows: 

Pr [ xt+1 = ed | x1, . . . , x t] = [softmax ( yt/τ )] d = exp ( yt,d /τ )

PDd′=1 exp ( yt,d ′ /τ ) , d = 1 , . . . , D.

Single-layer synthetic datasets. To generate the single-layer datasets, we instantiate one frozen teacher SSM for every combination of the input dimension D ∈ { 4, 8, 16 }, the state dimension 

N ∈ { 4, 8, 16 }, and the stability limit ρ ∈ { 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 0.95 , 0.99 , 0.995 , 0.999 } (from Defini-tion 4.1), which gives 3 × 3 × 8 = 72 different frozen teachers. Each such teacher, with its specific combination of D, N , and ρ, then generates 4,096 training and 1,024 test sequences of length S = 64 

according to the procedure described above.

A clamping projection onto Sγ,δ is available as a safeguard, but it was not active for any of the 72 accepted teachers. 

Sampling. Given the frozen teacher with a combination of D, N , and ρ and the frozen weights constructed as described above, a sequence is generated by drawing x1 uniformly from {e1, . . . , e D },starting from h0 = 0N ×D, and sampling xt+1 as described above for 79 further steps. The first 16 

tokens are discarded as burn-in and the remaining S = 64 tokens are stored as a single sequence. The burn-in aims to reduce the stored prefix’s dependence on the first input. The recurrent state at the start of the stored window is not persisted, and every subsequent teacher-forced or learner evaluation restarts from h0 = 0N ×D at the first stored token, which is the convention of the theory. The training and test splits are sampled with separate fixed seeds ( 1,000 and 1,001 ,003 ), reused in every configuration. Each dataset stores the token indices as 8-bit integers together with the frozen teacher parameters, the generation settings, and the stability certificate, and is never modified afterwards. The learner only sees the stored tokens and never accesses the teacher. 

Acceptance criteria. A generated candidate dataset is kept only if it passes the fixed criteria of Table 4, which exclude degenerate teachers (whose sequences collapse into a dominant token, or become deterministic, constant, or uninformative), while ensuring that the next-token task carries predictive information. All diagnostics of the table are computed on the union of the training and test splits, unless stated otherwise. The central quantity is the teacher-forced negative log-likelihood (NLL): the stored tokens of a sequence are fed to the teacher as inputs, starting from h0 = 0N ×D, instead of re-sampling from it, and at every position t = 1 , . . . , S − 1 we record − ln (Pr[ xt+1 | x1, . . . , x t]) ,i.e., minus the log-probability that the teacher (at the sampling temperature τ ) assigns to the stored next token. The NLL is the average of these values over all positions and sequences and measures how predictable the generated data are for the very model that produced them. A model that assigns probability 1/D to every token has NLL ln D, which serves as the uniform baseline. The informativeness criterion therefore requires the sequences to be predictable beyond chance, while the perplexity criterion exp(NLL) ≥ 1.20 excludes near-deterministic teachers. The mutual information is estimated from the empirical frequencies of consecutive token pairs. To avoid generating full datasets for clearly degenerate candidates, each candidate is first screened on a small draw of 256 

training and 128 test sequences, and only if this screen passes is the full dataset generated and checked again against the same thresholds, which are never relaxed. 

Gain and temperature choices. As ρ → 1, the hidden state of a random teacher accumulates input contributions over a horizon of order 1/(1 − ρ), so its logits grow and sampling collapses into a few tokens, which violates the balance and entropy criteria. Instead of relaxing the criteria, we search a fixed grid of input-projection gains and sampling temperatures, 

(g, τ ) ∈ (1 , 0.7) , (1 , 1) , (0 .5, 0.7) , (0 .5, 1) , (0 .25 , 0.7) , (0 .25 , 1) , (0 .15 , 0.7) ,

(0 .15 , 1) , (0 .1, 0.7) , (0 .1, 1) , (0 .05 , 0.7) , (0 .05 , 1) , (0 .03 , 1) ,

in this order for the teacher seeds s ∈ { 0, 1, 2} (outer loop), and accept the first candidate that passes every criterion in Table 4. Reducing g scales WB (and therefore ¯Bt) and the accumulated state without touching W∆ or A, so the margins (γ, δ ) and ρ remain unchanged. Over the 72 dataset 83 Preprint. Under review. (30 configurations), 0.5 (9), 0.25 (15 ), 0.15 (8), 0.1 (2), 0.05 (6), and 0.03 (2). A gain below one was required in every configuration with ρ ≥ 0.99 and in only 7 of the 27 configurations with ρ ≤ 0.7.The accepted temperatures were τ = 0 .7 (39 configurations) and τ = 1 (33 configurations). The resulting teachers improve on the uniform NLL by 0.02 to 0.97 , have a consecutive-token mutual information between 0.008 and 0.90 and a teacher perplexity between 1.5 and 15 .7. The most frequent token has frequency at most 1.76 /D , the normalized marginal entropy is at least 0.94 , and at least 98% of the stored sequences are unique.

Model Merging. For model merging, we evaluate on four text classification datasets: AG News (Zhang et al., 2015), DBpedia (Lehmann et al., 2015), TREC (Li and Roth, 2002), and IMDB (Maas et al., 2011). The dataset statistics and evaluation limits are summarized in Table 6. Each task is formulated as causal-LM scoring without a classification head. At evaluation time, we score prompt + ‘ ’ + label for every candidate label and select the label with the highest mean per-token log-probability over the label tokens. We use the mean, rather than the sum, so that labels of different lengths remain comparable. The evaluation pool is sub-sampled with a fixed seed of 99991 so that the evaluation split is identical across training seeds. The training pool is sub-sampled with the per-run training seed ( seed ∈ 0, 1, 2) so that the three runs differ both in optimization noise and in their training subsamples. Prompts are truncated or padded to max seq len = 256 , with 16 tokens reserved for the label completion and template tail. During tokenization, we construct a boolean label mask . This mask is used during fine-tuning to compute the loss only over label-token positions, and during evaluation to average scores only over label tokens. 85 Preprint. Under review. Synthetic Validation. The learner SSM has the exact same architecture as the teacher: a single-layer selective SSM as defined in Section 3, with the four parameter blocks W = ( W∆, W B , W C , A ) of sizes D×D, N ×D, N ×D, and N ×1, one-hot inputs, and no embedding, convolution, residual path, normalization, MLP, or Transformer components. The output yt ∈ RD is used directly as the logit vector for next-token prediction. In every configuration, the learner uses the same input dimension D

and state dimension N as the teacher that generated its dataset ( D must match the vocabulary), so its parameter count is D2 + 2 N D + N (Table 8). The learner never receives the teacher parameters -the only teacher-related quantities it uses are the margins (γ, δ ) of its configuration, which define the stability set Sγ,δ in which it is trained. Table 8: Learner parameter counts D2 + 2 N D + N for the synthetic configurations. 

N = 4 N = 8 N = 16 

D = 4 52 88 160 

D = 8 132 200 336 

D = 16 388 520 784 

Initialization. For a learner seed s ∈ { 0, 1, 2}, drawn independently of the teacher seed, the projections are initialized with i.i.d. Gaussian entries (W∆)d,d ′ , (WB )n,d , (WC )n,d ∼ N (0 , 0.12),and the transition block as 

An = − (γ + ϵ) − 0.1 |zn|, zn ∼ N (0 , 1) , n = 1 , . . . , N, 

with a safety margin ϵ = 10 −4. The initial parameters are then projected onto Sγ+ϵ,δ +ϵ by clamping 

An ≤ − (γ + ϵ) and (W∆)d,d ′ ≥ Softplus −1(δ + ϵ), and membership in Sγ,δ is verified over all 

D one-hot inputs, exactly as for the teachers. The initialization scale 0.1 is much smaller than the teacher’s 0.75 , so every learner starts close to a uniform next-token predictor. The same projection is applied after every optimizer update during training, so the learner remains in Sγ,δ with realized transitions bounded by ρ throughout training. All learner parameters are stored and updated in single precision ( float32 ).

Hybrid and Attention Backbones. The backbone ablation (see the ablations in the Practical Distributed Learning Framework subsection) replaces some or all of the four Mamba2 mixers of the depth-4 model by causal multi-head attention layers, keeping d model = 256 , the tied GPT-2 embedding and LM head, the RMSNorm layers, and the residual structure unchanged, so that the mixer of every layer is either a Mamba2 block with the configuration above or an attention block. Attention layers are mamba ssm ’s MHA (Multi-Head Attention) with 8 heads of dimension 32 , rotary position embeddings over the full head dimension, causal masking, biases on the input ( qkv ) and output projections, and no feed-forward sublayer. The attention layers are spread evenly through the stack. With nattn attention layers among the L = 4 layers, the j-th attention layer (counting from j = 0 ) is placed at index ⌊(j + 0 .5) L/n attn ⌋, which puts a single attention layer in the middle of the stack (index 2), two attention layers at indices 1 and 3, and three at indices 0, 2, and 3, with the remaining layers being Mamba2 mixers. To check that the placement itself does not drive the results, hybrid50alt keeps two attention layers but moves them to indices 0 and 2. Table 10 lists the resulting layouts and parameter counts: an attention layer has 263 ,168 parameters against 

465 ,176 for a Mamba2 mixer, so the total parameter count decreases with the attention fraction. The attention-only model ( attn0 ) serves as the zero-SSM control (since it has no SSM state to align).

For every configuration (D, N, ρ ) and learner seed s ∈ { 0, 1, 2}, the synthetic pipeline runs four stages on the dataset corresponding to that configuration: (i) centralized training of the learner of the Models subsection, (ii) evaluation of the gradient bound of Theorem 4.4 at the stored checkpoints, (iii) evaluation of the smoothness constants of Lemma 4.7 at the same checkpoints, and (iv) federated training with FedAvg and FedProx, which measures the quantities entering Theorem B.5 and Theo-rem B.6. Stages (ii) and (iii) never train, they only read checkpoints. All stages use the margins (γ, δ )

of the configuration. Table 12 lists the shared settings. Table 12: Shared settings of the synthetic centralized and federated runs.

Centralized Training. The learner minimizes the mean teacher-forced next-token cross-entropy over the S − 1 shifted positions of every training sequence, i.e., the sequence loss of (3) with ℓt the softmax cross-entropy and y∗ 

> t

= xt+1 . SGD uses no momentum, weight decay, or clipping, while projection and epoch reshuffling differ from the analyzed independent-sampling protocol. Each epoch shuffles the 4,096 training sequences with a seed derived from the learner seed and the epoch index and cuts them into batches of 64 , running for 2,000 updates. After every update the parameters are projected onto Sγ+ϵ,δ +ϵ as at initialization, and membership in Sγ,δ is re-verified over all D one-hot inputs. Checkpoints are written every 100 updates, and the test NLL, perplexity, and next-token accuracy are evaluated on the 1,024 test sequences at the same frequency, always restarting from h0 = 0N ×D at the first stored token. 

Gradient-Bound Evaluation. At the five checkpoints of Table 12 and at the frozen teacher, every one of the 1,024 test sequences is processed in float64 , and the gradient norm of its sequence loss with respect to the four blocks is computed as follows: ∥∇ W LS ∥F =   P

Lℓ = √2 (the gradient of the softmax cross-entropy with respect to the logits has norm at most √2). The tightened bound Gtight SSM (W ) uses realized stability margins, exact one-hot projection bounds, and finite-history sums: the realized margins ˆγ = min n(−An), ˆδ, and ˆρ = exp( −ˆγˆδ) replace 

(γ, δ, ρ ), max d ∥ Softplus( W∆ed)∥2 replaces K∆, the largest column norms max d ∥WB ed∥2 and

These replacements upper-bound the same one-hot projection and history terms and do not exceed the configured constants. A violation is recorded when the measured norm exceeds the bound by more than a relative tolerance of 10 −8. The utilization is the ratio of the measured norm to the bound. The gradient of the batch-mean loss over all test sequences is evaluated as well and checked against the mean of the per-sequence norms (triangle inequality) and against the bound. 

Smoothness Evaluation. At the same checkpoints, 32 test sequences drawn once with a fixed seed are processed in float64 . For every sequence, (a) the operator norm of the Hessian of the sequence loss is estimated by power iteration with exact Hessian-vector products (at most 15 iterations, stopping when the Rayleigh quotient changes by less than a relative 10 −9) and compared with LSSM ,R at 

R = 0 , (b) the finite-difference ratio ∥∇L S (W + hU ) − ∇L S (W )∥F /h with h = 10 −3 is evaluated along three unit directions U (two random and the normalized gradient) and compared with LSSM ,h 

whenever the ball of radius h is guaranteed to lie in Sγ,δ . Between consecutive learner checkpoints 

Wa → Wb, the per-sequence ratio max i ∥∇ ℓi(Wb) − ∇ ℓi(Wa)∥F /∥Wb − Wa∥F is compared with 

LSSM ,R centered at Wa with R = ∥Wb − Wa∥F , and |G SSM (Wb) − G SSM (Wa)|/∥Wb − Wa∥F with 

LGSSM ,R of Lemma B.1. The loss-regularity constants are Lℓ = √2 and Hℓ = 1 /2.

Federated Training. FedAvg and FedProx are run on the same learner with K clients holding disjoint, equal-size shards of the 4,096 training sequences and weights pk = 1 /K . Under the IID partition, the sequences are randomly permuted (seeded by the learner seed) and cut into K shards. Under the dominant-token partition, the sequences are first sorted by their most frequent token, then by the fraction of positions that token occupies, and then cut into K shards, so that clients differ systematically in token composition. Every client starts each round from the broadcast model and performs E local SGD updates with the settings of Table 12, each on a fresh batch of 64 sequences drawn with replacement from the client’s shard, followed by the same projection and verification as in centralized training. FedProx adds μ(W − W r ) to every local gradient, taking projected SGD steps on F rk from Section 3. The server forms W r+1 = P 

> k

pkW r,E k in float64 and applies the projection once more. All runs start from the centralized learner initialization of the same seed and use T = 60 rounds. Table 13 lists the settings: the base setting is run on all 72 configurations, and the one-factor variations of K, E, μ, and the partition are run on the 16 core configurations, namely the 

ρ axis at D = N = 8 and all (D, N ) pairs at ρ = 0 .95 . With three seeds this gives 1,200 federated runs. Table 13: Synthetic federated settings. The base setting is run on all 72 configurations, each variation changes one factor of the base setting and is run on the 16 core configurations.

f ∗ = 0 .G.4 PRACTICAL DISTRIBUTED LEARNING FRAMEWORK 

The federated framework uses K = 4 clients unless stated otherwise (see the client-count ablation) and initializes all clients from the same randomly initialized model θ0. At the start of every round 

r, the server broadcasts the global model θr, each client k runs E local optimization steps on its private data starting from θr , and returns its updated parameters θr,E k to a server module that performs algorithm-specific aggregation. After aggregation, the new global model θr+1 is evaluated on the union of all clients’ local validation data (server validation pool). The per-round per-client training perplexities and the server validation perplexity are saved. 

Shared Training Schedule. All algorithms are evaluated under the same overall optimization schedule. Across communication rounds, we keep the number of clients, participation rate, local optimizer, batch construction, sequence length, and random seeds fixed. The only differences across methods are the algorithm-specific server or client update rule and the corresponding method-specific hyperparameters. The shared hyperparameters are summarized in Table 14. Local training runs in single precision with mixed-precision ( fp16 ) autocast and dynamic loss scaling, and gradients are clipped after unscaling. FedSAM is the one exception and runs in full float32 . A fresh AdamW optimizer is created at the start of every round, so no optimizer state carries across rounds. Table 14: Shared training hyperparameters used across all federated algorithms. 

Component Value 

Number of clients, K 4

Participation rate 1.0; all clients participate each round Communication rounds, T 50 

Local steps per round, E {64 , 128 , 256 }

Per-step batch size 8

Sequence length, S 512 

Local optimizer AdamW, β1 = 0 .9, β2 = 0 .999 , ϵ = 10 −8

Local weight decay 0.01 

Local learning rate 10 −3

Gradient clipping Global ℓ2 norm clipping with threshold 1.0

Sampling Infinite RandomSampler with replacement Random seeds {0, 1, 2} for PyTorch and NumPy The following metrics are saved for every round: • Train Perplexity (per client). During each local step, the client computes the token-level cross-entropy of the next-token objective: the logits at positions 1, . . . , S − 1 are scored against the tokens at positions 2, . . . , S , with S = 512 . The pre-aggregation training perplexity of client k in round r is exp(CE) , where CE is the mean cross-entropy over the 

E local steps of the round. • Validation Perplexity . After aggregation, the global model θr+1 is evaluated on the server validation pool. We report exp(CE) with +∞ saturation on overflow. 90 Preprint. Under review. 

Federated Algorithms. We evaluate nine federated learning algorithms, all sharing the schedule of Table 14. Throughout, pk denotes the sample-count weight of client k, and since every client processes the same number of sequences per round, pk = 1 /K in all runs. • FedAvg (McMahan et al., 2017). After each client completes its E local AdamW updates, the server sets θr+1 = PKk=1 pkθr,E k =: ¯θr+1 . This method serves as the main baseline and introduces no algorithm-specific hyperparameters. • FedProx (Li et al., 2020). FedProx adds the uniform proximal penalty μ 

> 2

∥θ − θr ∥2 

> F

to the local objective, which discourages local models from drifting far from the broadcast model. Server aggregation is identical to FedAvg. • SCAFFOLD (Karimireddy et al., 2020). SCAFFOLD corrects each client’s local update with the difference between a server control variate cr and the client control variate crk: the local gradient is shifted by λc (cr − crk) with correction scale λc. The control variates are refreshed every fc rounds with control learning rate ηc, and their norm is bounded by τc.Aggregation of the model parameters is identical to FedAvg. • FedAdam (Reddi et al., 2021). Clients run the unchanged AdamW local phase. The server applies Adam to the pseudo-gradient ¯θr+1 − θr: it maintains first- and second-moment estimates mr and vr with coefficients (βs 

> 1

, β s 

> 2

) and updates θr+1 = θr + αs mr /(√vr + τs)

coordinatewise, with server learning rate αs and offset τs.• FedAlign (Kec ¸eci et al., 2025). FedAlign aligns the Mamba2 state coordinates of the client models before averaging. In the default mode ( align mode = mamba2 ref ), the 

B/C state features of every client are matched to the current global model as the reference within each group, applying the same permutation to all coupled parameters. Optionally (align mamba2 heads = 1 ), heads are matched as well, permuting every head-indexed parameter. After alignment, the client parameters are averaged with the same weights as FedAvg. • FedDyn (Acar et al., 2021). Each client keeps a persistent dual state νrk , initialized at zero, and trains the local objective Lk(θ) − ⟨ νrk , θ ⟩F + α 

> 2

∥θ − θr ∥2 

> F

, with the two regularization terms computed in float32 outside autocast. After local training, the client sets νr+1  

> k

=

νrk −α(θr,E k −θr ). The server keeps the average dual state νr+1 = νr −α P 

> k

pk(θr,E k −θr )

and updates θr+1 = ¯θr+1 − νr+1 /α . The local optimizer remains AdamW, and the gradient of the regularized objective is clipped like every other local gradient. • FedSAM (Qu et al., 2022). Every local step is a first-order sharpness-aware step: the client computes the gradient g at the current iterate θ, perturbs the weights by 

u = RSAM g/ (∥g∥F + 10 −12 ) with the norm taken jointly over all trainable parame-ters, computes the gradient at θ + u, restores θ exactly, and applies the perturbed gradient (clipped) with AdamW. Aggregation is identical to FedAvg. Because the perturbation must be formed from exact gradients across two backward passes, FedSAM runs without mixed precision at roughly twice the wall-clock cost of the other methods. • FedExP (Jhunjhunwala et al., 2023). Local training is identical to FedAvg. With the client displacements θr,E k − θr and their weighted average P
