The final reported metrics correspond, per configuration, to the hyperparameter value with the lowest validation perplexity averaged over the last 10 rounds (rounds 41-50) and over the three seeds. Runs that end with a non-finite perplexity are counted as failures and are never replaced. 

Reporting Metrics. For every (depth , E, β, setting ) configuration we report: • train ppl : the per-round, per-client training perplexity exp(CE) averaged over rounds and clients, then averaged over seeds (with its standard deviation). • val ppl : the server validation perplexity exp(CE) averaged over all 50 rounds, then over seeds (with its standard deviation). • val ppl last10 : the server validation perplexity averaged over rounds 41–50, then over seeds (with its standard deviation). 

Ablations. We further add two ablation experiments to the main grid, both at the constant settings 

β = 0 .7, depth 4, E = 64 , T = 50 with three seeds and one fixed hyperparameter value per algorithm: FedProx μ = 10 −3 (5×10 −4 in the backbone ablation), SCAFFOLD fc = 8 , FedAdam 

αs = 10 −2, FedAlign without head matching (with head matching in the backbone ablation), FedDyn 

α = 10 −2, FedSAM RSAM = 10 −2, FedExP ϵg = 10 −3, and DiLoCo-style (αo, β o) = (0 .7, 0.9) .The client-count ablation varies K ∈ { 4, 8, 16 , 32 } on all four domain settings. Clients are assigned primary domains round-robin over the four domains of the setting, so K/ 4 clients share each primary domain, and each client draws a fraction β of its data from its primary domain and (1 − β)/3 from each of the other three. Since the demand on every domain grows as K/ 4, the per-client budget is capped at the available domain pool divided by this demand: the 7,427 /1,857 training/validation sequences per client at K = 4 become approximately 3,713 /928 , 1,856 /464 , and 928 /232 at K = 8 ,

16 , and 32 for Settings 1 and 4, and slightly fewer for Settings 2 and 3 (which are limited by by the size of c4 ). The backbone ablation keeps K = 4 and Setting 1 and replaces some or all Mamba2 mixers of the depth-4 model by attention layers. The six backbones are described in the Models subsection. G.5 MODEL MERGING FRAMEWORK 

The merging framework is a two-phase pipeline. In the first phase, we fine-tune the pretrained base 

θ0 on each of the four downstream tasks independently, producing four task specialists θm. In the second phase, we merge the specialists with each configured method and evaluate the merged state on every task’s evaluation split. 

Per-Task Local Fine-Tuning For each task m ∈ { ag news , dbpedia 14 , trec , imdb } the pretrained base θ0 is fine-tuned on the task’s training pool with the causal-LM cross-entropy objective restricted to label-token positions. Specialists are checkpointed at every epoch in ”Checkpoint epochs” so that phase 2 can be repeated at multiple training horizons without re-paying the fine-tuning cost. Table 16 lists the optimization parameters. The cross-entropy loss is masked to label-token positions only, prompt and template tokens are ignored. The training-pool subsample varies with the seed (so each seed sees a different 40k subset 92 Preprint. Under review. Table 16: Per-task fine-tuning recipe (identical across tasks and seeds). 

Component Value 

Optimizer AdamW Learning rate 2 × 10 −4

Weight decay 0.0 Gradient clipping ℓ2 norm, threshold 1.0 Train batch size 4 for mamba2-130m, mamba2-370m , 8 for 

mamba2-780m 

Eval batch size 4LR schedule linear warmup + cosine decay Warmup ratio 0.05 (5% of total steps) Total epochs 4Checkpoint epochs {1, 2, 4}

Seeds per (model, task) {0, 1, 2}

Sequence length 256 tokens of the underlying task), while the evaluation-pool subsample is held fixed across seeds (eval seed = 99,991) so that all reported accuracies are comparable across seeds and methods. G.6 REPRODUCIBILITY 

Deterministic Data and Seed Handling. All three frameworks share a single PyTorch + NumPy seed. The federated harness deterministically caches the tokenized RedPajama subsets to a single binary cache file shared across all algorithms, settings, and seeds. The merging harness deterministically samples train and eval pools from each downstream dataset, with the eval seed (99,991) held fixed across all training seeds so that all reported accuracies are directly comparable. 

Hardware and Runtime. All reported experiments were run on single-GPU workers using NVIDIA A100-40GB and NVIDIA H100 GPUs. The A100-40GB workers were used for prototyping, de-bugging, and smaller validation runs, as well as the synthetic dataset generation and validation experiments. The remaining main sweeps of federated and model merging experiments were run on the H100 workers. Each run used one GPU worker, with host CPU/RAM used only for data loading, tokenization-cache access, logging, and checkpoint I/O. The tokenized RedPajama subsets were cached once and reused across algorithms, settings, and seeds. For federated language-modeling experiments, the dominant cost was local client training. Runtime scaled approximately linearly with the number of local steps E. In our setup, single federated runs ranged from approximately 30 minutes for E = 64 to at most roughly 2 hours for the longest 

E = 256 runs on an H100. All federated algorithms used the same base hardware and data pipeline. Their runtime differences were small relative to the effect of E. Naturally, the 16 -layer model scaled the computation compared to the 4-layer one. For model merging, each Phase-1 task-expert fine-tuning configuration required up to roughly 8 hours on a single H100, depending on checkpoint size. 

## H ADDITIONAL EXPERIMENTAL RESULTS AND ABLATION STUDIES 

H.1 SINGLE LAYER SYNTHETIC DISTRIBUTED LEARNING 

Note that we validate the single-layer statements only. The multi-layer bound of Theorem C.3 composes the same per-layer constants with the layerwise sensitivities κq of (303): the contribution of layer q is the single-layer constant of that layer (with its parameters adjusted to the multi-layer setting) multiplied by Q 

> j>q

κj over the layers above it. When κj ≥ 1, these factors do not reduce 93 Preprint. Under review. the per-layer bounds, but this does not establish greater relative looseness. Useful layers can also contract perturbations. Numerical evaluation is restricted to the single-layer theory.

Realized stability and training. Table 21 and Figure 5 demonstrate that the bounds use learners that generally outperform uniform prediction and approach the teachers’ test Negative Log Likelihood (NLL). At short memory ( ρ far away from 1), the teachers’ small predictive advantage over uniform makes normalized gap recovery sensitive to small absolute errors, while the learners remain well inside the stability limit. At long memory ( ρ approaches 1), learners nearly match the teachers and train close to that limit.

E0.87 while the initialization-based reference radius R = ηE GSSM (W 0) grows as E1 (Figure 6a). The client disagreement P

What drives the bound. Table 22 and Figure 9 give signed covariance attribution across the bank. The recurrent-history term carries at least 99% of the bound, so log 10 GSSM is the sum of log-factors ρ/ (1 − ρ)2, N D , ∥WC ∥2, KB , K∆, and K∆ + √D∥A∥2, plus a residual below 0.1

decades. Each share is the covariance of a log-factor with log-bound magnitude divided by the latter’s variance. The memory factor spans 6.2 decades and contributes 93% . Dimension, readout and step contribute 7% , 6% and 4% , while the transition factor contributes −13% , offsetting memory growth. This offset helps explain the exponent above −2 in Figure 2b. Teacher initialization gives 

∥A∥2 = γpP 

> n

(1 + 0 .25 |zn|)2 with γ = − ln ρ/δ . For log-utilization, memory contributes 102% 

and measured gradient growth −18% . Recurrence therefore dominates variation in tightness across this bank, not necessarily absolute looseness. Table 22: What drives the bound across the bank. log 10 GSSM is the sum of the logarithms of the factors of its recurrent-history term (plus a residual for the remaining terms), so the covariance of each factor with log 10 GSSM divided by its variance is an additive attribution that sums to one over all 1,296 parameter points. The last column attributes the variation of the utilization ∥∇L∥ F /GSSM in the same way, with the measured gradient as an additional term. A negative share means the factor moves against the total: ∥A∥2 shrinks as ρ → 1.

H.2 PRACTICAL DISTRIBUTED LEARNING 

In this subsection we provide supplemental results to the Section 5 Mamba2 federated learning experiments. Every table reports the three metrics defined in Appendix G: the client training perplexity, the server validation perplexity (both averaged over all 50 rounds), and the server validation perplexity averaged over the last 10 rounds. for FedAvg, FedAlign, FedSAM, and DiLoCo-style, by 52% for FedExP, and by 16% for FedAdam. The corresponding increases are only 9% for FedProx and 6% for SCAFFOLD. FedDyn instead improves by approximately 9% , although this comparison uses a separately selected α at each β.Increasing the number of local steps strengthens the relative advantages of FedDyn and SCAFFOLD. Their reductions in last-10 perplexity relative to FedAvg grow from approximately 12% and 4.5% 

at E = 64 to 22% and 10% at E = 256 , respectively. FedProx maintains a comparatively uniform reduction of 24 -27% across the three values of E. These trends are qualitatively consistent with the role of the E(E − 1) drift term in the bounds and the benefits of radius control or heterogeneity correction discussed in our analysis. Naturally, larger E results in longer training, so all nine algorithms achieve approximately 35 -45% lower last-10 perplexity when E increases from 64 to 256 

at fixed T = 50 communication rounds. The remaining methods generally remain close to, or underperform, FedAvg on the last-10 metric. FedAlign and FedSAM stay within 2% of FedAvg throughout the factor tables, while DiLoCo-style stays within 5% . All-round validation perplexity provides an important contrast to these last-10 results. Despite its strong late-training performance, FedDyn has substantially higher all-round validation perplexity than FedAvg, as does FedAdam. Across the factor tables, their all-round averages are approximately 

1.7-6.4 times those of FedAvg, reaching 814 .4 for FedDyn and 355 .2 for FedAdam at β = 1 . The round-wise results place these averages in context: after round 1, FedDyn and FedAdam have validation perplexities approximately 8 and 9 times that of FedAvg, respectively, using geometric means across configurations. FedDyn subsequently falls below FedAvg from round 4 onward and finishes 18% lower, whereas FedAdam remains above FedAvg throughout training and finishes 9% 

higher. Domain composition and model depth change the absolute perplexity levels without changing the ordering of the three leading methods. Setting 1 yields the lowest last-10 perplexity and Setting 4 the highest for every algorithm. For FedAvg, these values differ by approximately a factor of 

2.7. FedProx improves on FedAvg by approximately 24 -26% in each setting. Increasing depth from 4 to 16 reduces last-10 perplexity by approximately 15 -18% for FedAvg, FedAlign, FedSAM, FedExP, DiLoCo-style, and FedAdam, compared with 11% for SCAFFOLD, 8% for FedProx, and 

4% for FedDyn. Consequently, the relative improvements over FedAvg narrow from 28% to 22% for FedProx, from 22% to 12% for FedDyn, and from 9% to 4% for SCAFFOLD. H.2.2 CLIENT -C OUNT ABLATION

client counts ( 60 .9 and 61 .1). The training perplexity continues to decrease, from 144 at K = 4 to 

129 at K = 32 , potentially reflecting more frequent reuse of smaller local datasets. The three leading methods in the main grid -FedProx, FedDyn, and SCAFFOLD - remain the strongest on last-10 validation perplexity at every K. FedAlign and FedSAM remain within 1% of FedAvg, whereas FedAdam has 8-9% higher perplexity. FedProx maintains a reduction of 22 -24% compared to FedAvg across the evaluated values of K. In contrast, the reductions achieved by SCAFFOLD and FedDyn increase from 3% to 13% and from 7% 

to 18% , respectively, between K = 4 and K = 32 . DiLoCo-style moves from 3% higher perplexity than FedAvg to 8% lower, while FedExP moves from approximate parity to a 6% reduction. As such, although the leading methods remain unchanged, their relative gains and the ordering of the remaining methods vary with client count. One possible explanation for these method-dependent trends is that having multiple clients per primary domain at larger K produces a more stable aggregate update than the one-client-per-domain partition at K = 4 . However, this interpretation is not directly tested by the ablation. Increasing K

also changes the total client work, local dataset sizes, and data partitioning. These results therefore describe performance under the evaluated client-count protocol at a single (β, depth , E ) setting, rather than isolating the effect of client count or establishing a particular algorithmic mechanism. The server-step methods are the second exception: their penalty grows as Mamba2 layers are replaced, from +8% (FedAdam) and +1% (DiLoCo-style) on mamba2 to +40% and +14% on 

attn0 , whereas FedExP, neutral on mamba2 , is 3-6% better than FedAvg on every backbone that contains attention. H.3 MODEL MERGING 

This subsection reports the one-shot merging experiments referenced in Appendix F. Following the framework of Appendix G.5, the pretrained Mamba2-130M, 370M, and 780M checkpoints (Table 11) are fine-tuned separately on AG News, DBpedia, TREC, and IMDB (Table 6) with the recipe of Table 16, and the four specialists θm = θ0 + Tm saved after 1, 2, and 4 epochs are merged by weight averaging ( pm = 1 /4), Task Arithmetic ( θ0 + λ P

m Tm), and TIES-Merging (trim each task vector to the fraction d of its largest-magnitude entries, elect signs, average the agreeing entries, and scale the result by λ). Every merged model is evaluated on all four tasks by causal-LM label scoring, and we report the accuracy averaged over the four tasks (mean ± standard deviation over three fine-tuning seeds), as well as the accuracies of the pretrained model and the specialists evaluated on their own and on the other tasks. Task Arithmetic and TIES-Merging are shown at the best of the evaluated 

λ and (λ, d ) for each checkpoint and epoch. Tables 31 and 32 give the full sweeps, Table 30 the per-task accuracies after four epochs, and Table 33 a variant of the 130M experiment in which the specialists are fine-tuned with the FedProx proximal term. 

Comments. After four epochs, specialists average 89 -91% accuracy on their own tasks but show little transfer to the other tasks. Task Arithmetic and TIES-Merging outperform weight averaging, with the 101 Preprint. Under review. Table 28: Backbone ablation. best evaluated merges reaching 61 .3, 71 .8, and 72 .1% for the 130M, 370M, and 780M checkpoints, respectively, compared with 37 .4, 50 .4, and 51 .7% for averaging (Table 29). Larger checkpoints narrow the gap to specialists, although substantial deficits remain, especially on DBpedia and TREC (Table 30). The sweeps favor Task Arithmetic scales of λ = 0 .7-1, rather than the averaging scale 

λ = 0 .25 , and sparse, rescaled TIES updates, with (λ, d ) = (1 .3, 0.1) performing best among the four-epoch settings evaluated (Tables 31 and 32). Additionally, fine-tuning with the proximal penalty 

μ

2 ∥θ − θ0∥2

F reduces four-epoch averaging accuracy from 37 .4% at μ = 0 to 26 .2% at μ = 10 −1,near the pretrained baseline (Table 33). 

Relation to the analysis. These results are consistent with the trade-off in the local loss control (466) between the linear alignment term ⟨∇ f (θ0), θ merge − θ0⟩F and the quadratic smoothness remainder. Weight averaging preserves neighborhood containment but rescales each task vector by 1/M . For the 780M model parameters, ∥ P

m Tm∥2

F / P

m ∥Tm∥2

F = 1 .04 -1.07 , indicating little net alignment among the task vectors. Together with the scale sweeps, this suggests that averaging suppresses useful task-specific adaptation: larger displacements can improve accuracy despite weaker containment guarantees, illustrating why containment alone does not ensure predictive performance (Appendix F.1). The proximal results suggest a similar trade-off. Limiting specialist drift can also suppress useful adaptation, and, unlike iterative federated learning, one-shot merging has no subsequent rounds to recover it. Finally, the uniform smoothness bound does not explain the larger accuracy gaps on DBpedia and TREC, motivating further investigation of the sensitivity-aware per-block scales discussed in Appendix F.2. 102 Preprint.
